A *move job* is a move that goes on over as many runs as it takes: it moves what fits on the disks attached now, stops, says which disk to attach next, and carries on by itself when one is attached. Optionally the data then leaves the cloud storage, once the pool holds the copies the policy needs.

**Commands.**

- `varsto move <folder> --from <storage> --to <pool|storage> [--path <dir-or-file>]... [--idle-days N] [--keep-source] [--job] [--dry-run] [--confirm-cold-read] [--yes]`. A move to a pool, or with `--path`, `--keep-source` or `--job`, is recorded as a job and run once with the disks attached now; other moves behave as in section 23. `--path` (repeatable) limits the job to the files at or under those paths, which are relative to the folder or absolute inside its directory on this device; a path that holds no current file is refused. `--keep-source` copies and checks but never drops from `--from`. The same request while its job is open continues that job instead of starting another. The CLI asks (with the plan's summary) unless `--yes`, and exits with code 1 when blocks of a finished job stay on the source.
- `varsto move status [id]` lists open and paused jobs (or one job, finished or not) with bytes done, bytes left and the disk each waits for; `varsto move pause <id>`; `varsto move resume <id>` (opens a paused job and runs it now); `varsto move cancel <id>`. These need no passphrase except `resume`. Cancelling never deletes anything: what was moved stays moved and what was copied stays copied, so the ledger and the storages stay consistent; a new job for the same folder picks up the rest.
- `varsto status` lists the open jobs under the folders, one line each (`status --json` adds `move_jobs` when the service answers). `GET /api/moves` lists the jobs; `POST /api/move/state {id, action: pause|resume|cancel}` changes one; the service summary has `move_jobs`.

**The job file.** `<home>/state/moves/<id>.json`, `id` = `mv-` and eight hex digits, written atomically after every group of blocks and at the end of every run: `folder` (name) and `folder_id`, `paths`, `from`, `to`, `idle_days`, `keep_source`, `confirm_cold_read`, `policy` (the folder's copy rules when it started, in words), `state` (`open`, `paused`, `done`, `cancelled`), `bytes_total`, `bytes_done`, `bytes_remaining`, `blocks_total`, `blocks_done`, `need_second_place_bytes`, `disks_used` (labels), `started_utc`, `last_progress_utc`, `finished_utc`, `runs`, `waiting` (`kind` disk, place or room; `disk`, the known disk with room that continues the job; `text`), `room_sig`, `room_needed`, `kept_blocks` and `errors` (the last 20). The file holds no block names. What is left to move is worked out from the ledger at every run, so the counters are informative: a run recomputes `bytes_remaining` and corrects `bytes_done` (done = total - left), and a crash, a restart or another device finishing part of the move changes nothing but the numbers. A process that runs a job holds `<id>.lock` (created exclusively, refreshed after every group, taken over when older than ten minutes), so the service and the command line never move the same blocks at once.

**A run.** It pulls the ledger, plans the move as in section 23 (limited to `paths`; with `keep_source` the folder's copy rules do not keep blocks, nothing is dropped), then moves the blocks in groups of 64: write to the target unless it has the block, read back and hash-check, record `chunk_stored` and `chunk_verified` for the target, and (unless `keep_source`) record `chunk_dropped` for the source, seal and push the batch, then delete from the source, exactly as in section 23. The source copy of a group's block is dropped only when the target copy is checked and the policy still holds. A pool target is written through the pool itself (section 19): a block that finds no attached disk with room within the reserve, or no disk at all, is neither copied nor kept; the run notes the size and skips every block at least that large without reading it, and goes on with smaller blocks. A storage at its quota (section 44) waits the same way. When a run has moved what fits, the job stays `open` with `waiting`: `kind: "disk"` and the label of the known disk with the most room (its last measurement, section 40) or "attach a new disk" when no known disk has room. The service limits a run to 16 groups and goes on at its next turn.

**Disk groups.** For a pool with a `copies` rule (section 23) a block counts as moved only when the pool holds it in that many different places. After writing, the run adds copies on attached disks of other places; while a block is held in fewer places its source copy stays and the block is counted in `need_second_place_bytes` (`waiting.kind` is `place`: "N GB need a copy on a disk kept in another place"). With the second disk attached the next run adds the copies and drops the sources. With `keep_source` a block counts as done under the same rule.

**Done.** A job is done when nothing in its scope is left waiting. Blocks that stay on the source because the folder's policy needs the copy there, a cold read is not confirmed, or no readable copy was found are listed in `errors` and `kept_blocks`; they do not hold the job open. A finished whole-folder job (no `paths`, no `idle_days`, no `keep_source`, nothing kept) also replaces the source with the target in the folder's placement, as a whole-folder move does (section 23), so new blocks go to the pool too. Until then the folder's placement should name the source, otherwise `disk add` and pushes would fill the pool with the whole folder by themselves.

**Automatic continuation.** The background service (every 30 seconds, with its disk listing, and after every sync) asks which open jobs are ready and runs them within its share. A job to a pool is ready when an attached disk has at least `room_needed` bytes (the smallest block that found no room) and the attached disks with their room differ from what the job saw when it last waited (`room_sig`): a disk was attached (`disk add`, `disk insert`, `disk check`, a mount appearing) or a disk's space changed (blocks freed, files deleted). A job to an ordinary storage is ready after every sync. A paused service, a locked vault and paused or finished jobs run nothing. `disk add`, `disk init`, `disk insert` and `disk check` say which jobs can continue with the disks attached (`varsto move resume <id>` does it at once).

**Next disk to dock.** `Engine::disk_suggestions` (section 40) takes the open jobs that wait for room on a pool (`moves`: id, folder, pool, `bytes_left`, `need_second_place_bytes`, `room_needed`). A disk of that pool whose free space within its reserve is at least `room_needed` gets a reason of kind `move`: "continue the move of <folder>: N GB left; this disk has M GB free", worth `65 × min(room / bytes left, 1)` points (at least the suggestion threshold), so a known disk that can continue a job is suggested before one that is merely overdue for a check; attached disks with room are listed apart as before. When no attached or known disk of the pool has room, `new_disk_needed` lists the job with "a new disk is needed" and the commands `varsto disk init <device> --pool <pool> --label <name>` or `varsto disk add <mount> --pool <pool> --label <name>`; `varsto disk suggest` prints them.

**Interface and events.** The Storages page shows, in each pool card, a line per waiting job with the bytes left, the bytes that need a second place and "Attach disk «X»" or "A new disk is needed"; a banner under the page header lists the waiting jobs from the service summary. The Help page (section 41) has a step and an attention line per waiting job. The event stream (section 54) has `move.waiting` (`id`, `folder`, `to`, `kind`, `disk`, `bytes_left`, `need_second_place_bytes`, `text`), sent when a run ends waiting with a different reason than the run before, and `move.done` (`id`, `folder`, `from`, `to`, `bytes`, `disks`). The service raises a desktop notification for both.

**Limits.** Jobs are per device (`<home>/state`): another device does not see them, though it sees the moved blocks in the ledger. The room of a pool is the free space the file system reports; a disk whose reserve leaves less than the smallest remaining block counts as full. A group rule needs disks in enough different places to exist; a job cannot finish with fewer. Moving a block to a pool does not change which disks a later `disk check` fills.

Tests: `crates/varsto-core/tests/staged_move.rs` (a folder moved to a pool disk by disk with the suggestion and the service trigger, `--keep-source`, `--path`, a disk group rule needing two places, pause and cancel, a job resumed after the engine is dropped between groups, an ordinary storage target and the run lock), `crates/varsto-core/src/disksuggest.rs` (ranking for waiting jobs), `crates/varsto-cli/tests/cli_smoke.rs` (`staged_move_jobs_through_the_cli`) and the scenario `staged-move-to-pool` of the lab (`scripts/test/lab/scenarios.py`).
## 59. Service and transport hardening

Changes that tighten the local service, the peer transport and the reads from storages. None of them changes an object or record on a storage.

**STUN side channel (section 52).** The shared UDP socket queues a datagram that looks like STUN only while `Node::stun` has a request open, only when bytes 8 to 20 equal the transaction id of that request, and into a bounded queue of 64 entries (`try_send`). Everything else is dropped without allocating and counted. Before, any host on the internet could grow the queue of an unbounded channel without limit by sending STUN-shaped datagrams to the UDP port. Test: `p2p::quic::tests::unsolicited_stun_flood_is_not_queued`.

**Session token out of addresses and argv (S3).** The service no longer hands the browser an address with the session token. `varsto desktop`, the tray, `varsto open-link`, the service's own browser start and the FFI `varsto_url` open `http://127.0.0.1:<port>/?boot=<code>`: a code of 32 random bytes (64 hex digits) that is valid for 60 seconds and works once (`POST /api/session/boot`, authenticated by the token header, mints it; at most 256 codes are kept). The page trades it with `POST /api/session` `{"boot": "<code>"}` for `{"token": …}`, keeps the token in `sessionStorage`, and removes the parameter from the address at once; a second use or an expired code answers 401. The token stays what it was: random per service start, written to `service.json` (0600), read by the CLI, the tray and the MCP server from there. The `?token=` parameter is not accepted on any route. The token is read from `X-Varsto-Token` or `Authorization: Bearer`. Thumbnails are fetched by the page with the header and shown from blob URLs. A download (`/api/open`) or viewer (`/api/view`) link, which a browser opens without headers, carries `ticket=<random>` from `POST /api/ticket` `{route, folder, path}`: the ticket opens exactly that route, folder and path, for ten minutes, any number of times (a video asks for ranges). Every answer under `/api/` has `Cache-Control: no-store`. The FFI adds `varsto_token()` for the mobile shells' own API calls. Tests: `desktop::tests::boot_code_opens_the_session_once`, `expired_boot_code_is_refused`, `the_token_is_never_accepted_in_the_address`, `tickets_open_exactly_one_file`.

**Request handling (S2).** Query values are decoded on bytes (`%XX` with two ASCII hex digits, else the byte as it is), so a malformed escape such as `%aé` cannot split a character; a panic in a request handler answers 500 and the API worker carries on (before, the one worker thread died and every stateful route hung). Test: `desktop::tests::percent_decode_survives_malformed_escapes`.

**Strongroom key commitment (S4, section 17).** See section 17: `check_hex`, `-t pin=true`, `--uv`. Test: `crates/varsto-core/tests/strongroom.rs::a_wrong_folder_key_is_refused_by_unlock_convert_and_rekey`.

**Passphrases and secrets off the command line (S5, section 28).** A device passphrase must be at least 12 characters after NFKC normalisation (`crypto::check_passphrase`, checked by `Keys::save` and before `init`, `join`, `pair`, `share accept` and `org join` create anything; the interface's forms say "At least 12 characters"). Keys already written with a shorter passphrase still open. Nothing secret is taken from the command line any more, because every process of the user can read it (`ps`, `/proc/<pid>/cmdline`) and the shell keeps it in its history. The options `join --vault-key` and `--words`, `--s3-secret-access-key` (`join`, `share accept`), `storage add-s3 --secret-access-key`, `mcp --key`, the positional shares of `recovery combine` and `org admin add|remove --root-words` are gone. Each secret now comes from, in order: a file named by `--key-file` (`join`; the file holds 64 hex digits or the 24 words), `--s3-secret-file`, `--file` (`recovery combine`, one share per line), `--root-words-file`, `--key-file` (`mcp`); the environment variable `VARSTO_VAULT_KEY`, `VARSTO_S3_SECRET`, `VARSTO_ORG_ROOT_WORDS`, `VARSTO_MCP_KEY`; standard input when it is not a terminal (`join`, `recovery combine`, `org admin`, `share accept`, `storage add-s3`); otherwise a prompt without echo. On macOS the passphrase is stored in the login keychain by feeding `add-generic-password` to `security -i` on standard input instead of `-w <passphrase>` in the arguments. Tests: `crates/varsto-cli/tests/cli_smoke.rs::secrets_stay_off_the_command_line` (a short passphrase refused with nothing written, the removed options refused, `join` reading the key from standard input), `main::tests` (key text parsing). The recovery guide, the kit text and the lab scenarios use the new forms.

**Updater freeze and rollback protection (S6, section 14).** A download site that serves an old signed release, or withholds a newer one, cannot make a client install the old one. (a) `manifest.json` must carry a valid release signature (`manifest.json.minisig`, trusted comment `file:manifest.json`, the version it names must equal `_version`); an unsigned manifest is refused. (b) The signing time in the trusted comment of `SHA256SUMS.sig` must not be older than the newest one accepted before, kept in `update-state.json` in the device directory (`{last_timestamp, last_version}`, moved forward only after the archive passed every check); an older one is refused as a rollback. A signed list more than 90 days old is accepted with a warning, since it may simply be the newest release. (c) `MIN_ACCEPTED_VERSION` is the version compiled into the binary: a version equal to or older than it is never installed. (d) The GitHub cross-check is required: when the release list there cannot be fetched, the update is postponed with the reason, and `varsto update --allow-single-source` goes on with the signature alone (the interface never does). `scripts/sign-release.sh` signs `manifest.json` next to `SHA256SUMS` and `--verify` checks both; `website/deploy.sh` refuses to publish a manifest without a valid signature. Tests: `update::tests` (`update_refuses_a_checksum_list_older_than_the_last_accepted`, `the_manifest_must_carry_the_release_signature`, `the_same_or_an_older_version_is_never_installed`, `update_waits_when_github_does_not_answer`, `a_signed_list_that_is_months_old_is_reported`).

**Bounded storage reads and pack index validation (S7, sections 28 and 30).** No read from a storage can make the program allocate more than the object kind allows. `Storage::get_bounded(key, max)` refuses an object larger than `max`; `get` is `get_bounded` with `storage::read_limit(key)`: `MAX_CHUNK_SIZE + 64` (16 MiB and 64 bytes) for `chunks/` and `packs/`, 256 MiB for records (`vault/`, `manifests/`, `ledger/`, `org/` and the rest). The local directory and pool disks check the file size before reading and read at most one byte past the limit; S3 refuses a `Content-Length` over the limit before reading the body, caps the body it reads (it was 2 GiB for every request) and uses checked arithmetic for ranges; rclone is run with `--count max+1` and its output is read through a cap (a listing may be up to 1 GiB), and the process is killed when it runs past it. A block read in the engine (fetch, scrub, verify, repair) is bounded by `MAX_CHUNK_SIZE + 64`. A claimed range (`offset`, `len` of a pack member) is clamped to a block object, with overflow-checked addition. `PackIndex::validate(key)` checks an index read from a storage: its pack id is 64 lowercase hex digits and equals the id in the index's own key, at most 4096 members, every member name is 64 lowercase hex digits (names were sliced with `[..2]`, which panics in the middle of a multi-byte character), `0 < len <= MAX_CHUNK_SIZE + 64`, and `offset + len` does not overflow and is at most 64 MiB. An index that fails is skipped, logged and counted (`PackAware::invalid_indexes`): not trusted, not a panic. A panic while reading one object during scrub, verify, repair or a fetch (`storage::guarded`) is an error for that object, not the end of the pass. Tests: `packs::tests::an_index_that_lies_is_rejected_not_trusted`, `hostile_indexes_in_a_storage_do_not_panic_a_listing`, `s3::tests::a_response_that_claims_two_gib_is_refused_without_reading_it`, `a_block_read_is_capped_at_a_chunk_not_a_record`, `storage::bounded_read_tests`.

**restic details out of logs and support bundles (S8, sections 35 and 45).** The log scrubber cuts the login of every URL: `scheme://user:password@host/...` becomes `scheme://[redacted]@host/...` (`redact::scrub`), so `rest:https://u:p@h/r` and `s3:https://K:S@h/b` never reach a log line, wherever they came from. The repository is not logged at all: a debug line for a restic call names the repository's kind and host only (`restic::repository_summary`: `rest:https://h`, `sftp:nas`). Messages, `varsto restic status` and the recovery kit's note show the repository without its login (`restic::display_repository`), and the status shows only the first word of a password command (the rest of a command line may hold the password). The `config.json` copy in a support bundle (`bundle_extras`, `redact::redact_config_json`) has `restic.password`, `restic.env_file` and every `access_key_id` and `secret_access_key` replaced by `[redacted]`, and the login of every URL cut; it is no longer copied as it is. Tests: `redact::tests::url_userinfo_is_cut_everywhere`, `a_config_copy_for_a_bundle_has_no_credentials`, `restic::tests::repositories_are_shown_without_their_login`, `restic` (CLI) `tests::status_and_kit_show_no_login_and_no_password_command_arguments`.

**MCP keys (S9, section 37).** The time a key was last used is kept in `mcp-keys-used.json` (`{key id: seconds}`, at most one write a minute), not in `mcp-keys.json`: checking a key reads the key file and never writes it, so a check running while `revoke` writes the file can no longer put the revoked key back (it used to read the whole file, set the time and write it back). A revoked key's use record goes with it. `varsto mcp` without a key is refused while the service runs ("the MCP server then needs an MCP key"): the session token would give the assistant everything; with no service it still opens the vault itself with `VARSTO_PASSPHRASE`. `GET /api/status` with a scoped key answers `{"folders": [...]}` with the folders the key may read and nothing else: no devices, no storages, no vault identity. Tests: `mcp_keys::tests::a_check_never_rewrites_the_keys_and_cannot_resurrect_a_revoked_key`, `crates/varsto-cli/tests/mcp.rs` (`a_scoped_key_reaches_only_its_folders_through_the_service`).

**Names that cannot redraw the terminal (S10, sections 27, 46 and 51).** `names::check_display_name` refuses a name of a device or a person with a control character (`ESC[2A` moves the cursor and redraws the fingerprint line), a zero-width character (U+200B to U+200F, U+2060 to U+2064, U+FEFF), a line or paragraph separator, a bidirectional control (U+202A to U+202E, U+2066 to U+2069, U+061C), or more than 64 characters. It runs when a name is made (device names at `init`, `join`, `pair`, `share accept` and `org join`; the names bound into a share request or an organization request) and again when a name is read from a code (`ShareRequest::parse`, `OrgRequest::parse`, a QR link), because a code is made by someone else. The command line shows every name through `names::display` (the same characters, if one gets in anyway, as `\u{..}` escapes): the QR caption, the approval questions and the member table. The page puts names in with `textContent`. Tests: `names::tests::display_names_refuse_control_invisible_and_direction_characters`, `vault::tests::request_codes_with_control_or_direction_characters_are_refused`, `org::tests::request_names_with_control_or_direction_characters_are_refused`.

**Disk markers carry a MAC (S11, section 34).** `.varsto-disk.json` holds the pool id and the vault tag in plain text on every disk of a pool, so anyone who has held one disk could copy the two fields under a new disk id and make a disk that every device of the vault adopted (and, with the most free space, chose as the favourite write target). A marker now also carries `mac_hex = keyed_hash(derive(root, "disk-pool-marker"), aad("disk-pool-marker", pool_id, disk_id, created_utc))` (length-prefixed fields; `aad` is the same framing as elsewhere). A disk that is not in the pool's registry is adopted on sight only when its MAC is valid (constant-time comparison); a marker with the pool id and vault tag but no valid MAC makes it an unknown disk: it gets no writes and is not in the registry, `varsto disk list` names it ("unknown disk ... carries no valid marker MAC ... run `varsto disk add <mount> --pool <pool> --label <label>`"), and `varsto disk add` (also `init` and `insert`, which register or re-attach drives) takes it in on purpose and writes the MAC; `disk list` and a re-attach add the MAC to the marker of a known disk that predates it. A copy of a real marker with another disk id fails the MAC (the disk id is in the message), as does the real MAC copied along with it. A disk already in the registry is recognised by its id as before. Tests: `pool::tests::a_copied_marker_without_a_valid_mac_is_never_adopted`, `crates/varsto-core/tests/disk_pool.rs::a_disk_with_a_copied_marker_is_not_adopted_until_the_user_adds_it`.
